Shell cwd was reset to ~/files/r/packages/rray4

Dropping v_loc_start and the it->end != 0 check together closes the gap. The leaner iterator now matches the macro on short runs, to within about 2.5%.

I added two versions to the variant package, both using the same loops as run_n:

  • lean: one location array that next() advances directly. No v_loc_start and no per-run copy. It still has the it->end != 0 check.

  • advance: also no copy, and no first-run check either. init already sets up the first run, and the loop advances at the end of each run, the same shape as today's macros:

    struct rray_run_iterator_n_advance it = rray_run_iterator_n_advance(plan, 2);

for (; !rray_run_iterator_n_advance_done(&it);
     rray_run_iterator_n_advance_next(&it, 2)) {
  ...
}

    done() is just it->start == it->size. next() runs RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT_N() and moves start / end along. After the last run it carries the point back to zero one extra time, which is harmless because nothing reads it.

Both pass the 262 test expectations and give identical() results to the macro.

Short-run cases (a million runs of 10 elements), median of 3 rounds of 50 iterations each, with the version order rotated between rounds:

Case Macro Run split Run n Lean Advance
Broadcast [10, 1] to [10, 1e6] 7.08 7.14 7.06 7.13 7.16
Broadcast [1, 1e6] to [10, 1e6] 7.00 7.09 7.39 7.22 7.32
Add [10, 1e6] + [10, 1] 8.36 9.12 9.60 8.91 8.57
Add [10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6] 7.98 8.28 8.70 8.08 7.81
Add, integer, [10, 1e6] + [10, 1] 12.08 12.58 13.13 13.18 12.02
Add, integer, [10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6] 14.46 15.16 14.50 14.79 14.36
Sum [10, 1e6] over 1 3.75 2.82 3.03 2.91 3.06
Sum [10, 1e6] over 2 4.02 3.99 3.99 3.99 3.92

What this shows:

  • Add shows the gap, and both changes help. advance is within 2.5% of the macro in all four add cases, and slightly faster in three of them. The old run_n was 3% to 15% slower.

  • Dropping the copy alone helps double add. On double add, lean gets about two thirds of the way there. On integer add it's mixed, and one case got no better. Removing the first-run check as well is what closes the gap consistently.

  • Broadcast and sum barely notice. The three newer iterator versions are all within noise of each other on broadcast, and all of them beat the macro on the short sum, thanks to the local total.

  • The machine was noisier this session. Long-run cases came out about 7% slower than in earlier runs. So I've only compared versions measured in the same run, and I wouldn't read anything into differences under about 3%.

The cost is the API shape: a for loop with done() and next() instead of a single while (next()). That's slightly less tidy, but it's the same structure the macros already use.

Want me to add these results to plans/iterators.md (recommending the advance shape and answering the per-run cost open question) and commit and push?

15:- The same run iterator, with each function writing one loop per stride case (an operand fixed or moving), matches the macros in nearly every case we measured. The exception is very short runs: adding `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` (a million runs of 10 elements) is 4% to 6% slower, in every one of six runs. It's faster in one case: summing `[10, 1e6]` over axis 1 takes 2.75 ms against 3.53 ms, because the total stays in a local variable.

Read plans/iterators.md 
13	- An iterator that hands back one run at a time, used with a single loop over the run, matches the macros when every operand moves along the run. When one operand stays fixed, it's 1.3x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting and addition, and 4x to 7x slower on sums.
14	
15	- The same run iterator, with each function writing one loop per stride case (an operand fixed or moving), matches the macros in nearly every case we measured. The exception is very short runs: adding `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` (a million runs of 10 elements) is 4% to 6% slower, in every one of six runs. It's faster in one case: summing `[10, 1e6]` over axis 1 takes 2.75 ms against 3.53 ms, because the total stays in a local variable.
16	
17	- One run iterator can serve any number of inputs, replacing today's separate one-input and two-input plans. It's as fast as the specialized ones, as long as each function passes the number of inputs as a literal (`1`, `2`) and the call is inlined. With the count known only at runtime, short runs get 25% to 75% slower.
18	
19	- That last design is what NumPy does. It is also what the macros already do. The loops are the same, so the speed is the same. What changes is where the loops live: in each function, where they can be read and changed, instead of inside a macro.
20	
21	- The cost is repetition. rray4 has 84 `RRAY_BINARY()` calls, 32 `RRAY_REDUCE()` calls and 5 `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` calls. Writing every stride case out by hand in each of them is a lot of code. How to handle the per-type repetition is the main open question.
22	
23	Recommendation: adopt the run iterator with per-stride-case loops, and decide separately how to avoid writing every type out by hand.
24

+- That design is what NumPy does. It is also what the macros already do. The loops are the same, so the speed is the same. What changes is where the loops live: in each function, where they can be read and changed, instead of inside a macro.
+
+- One run iterator can serve any number of inputs, replacing today's separate one-input and two-input plans. It's as fast as the specialized ones, as long as each function passes the number of inputs as a literal (`1`, `2`) and the call is inlined. With the count known only at runtime, short runs get 25% to 75% slower.

+- The short-run gap goes away when the iterator does less per run: keep one location array that the iterator advances directly (no per-run copy for the caller), and advance at the end of each run instead of checking for the first run at the start, like the macros do. That version is within 2.5% of the macro on every short-run case.
+
+- The cost is repetition. rray4 has 84 `RRAY_BINARY()` calls, 32 `RRAY_REDUCE()` calls and 5 `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` calls. Writing every stride case out by hand in each of them is a lot of code. How to handle the per-type repetition is the main open question.
+
+Recommendation: adopt one run iterator for any number of inputs, in the "advance at the end" shape, with per-stride-case loops written in each function. Decide separately how to avoid writing every type out by hand.
The file ~/files/r/packages/rray4/plans/iterators.md has been updated successfully.
Read plans/iterators.md 
532	With `RRAY_MAX_INPUTS` raised to 64, so `rray_index()` could use it, `run_n` was unchanged: for example 2.84 ms on the short sum along axis 1 and 6.16 ms on the short broadcast. The compiler still keeps the used slots in registers.
533	
534	## What the numbers say
535	
536	- With a literal count, one iterator for any number of inputs is as fast as the separate one-input and two-input versions. The small differences between `run_split` and `run_n` move in both directions between runs.
537	
538	- A count known only at runtime costs 25% to 75% on short runs, and nothing on long runs. The loops over inputs run once per run, so they only matter when there are millions of runs.
539	
540	- So the plan structs can be merged into one, as long as every function passes its count as a literal. Code like `rray_index()`, where the count really is decided at runtime, keeps its `switch` on the count, with a literal in each case.

-# The cost: repetition
+- So the plan structs can be merged into one, as long as every function passes its count as a literal. Code like `rray_index()`, where the count really is decided at runtime, keeps its `switch` on the count, with a literal in each case.

+
+# Removing the per-run cost
+
+`rray_run_iterator_n` does two things per run that the macros don't:
+
+- It keeps two location arrays. `v_loc_start` is what `next()` advances, and `v_loc` is a copy made at the start of every run for the caller. The copy only protects against a caller that changes `it.v_loc[i]` while walking the run. No caller does: every loop copies the start into a local (`r_ssize x_loc = it.v_loc[0];`) and moves the local.
+
+- `next()` checks `it->end != 0` on every run, so that the first call doesn't advance.
+
+## Two leaner iterators
+
+`rray_run_iterator_n_lean` keeps one array, `v_loc`, which `next()` advances directly. The caller reads it and never writes it. C can't enforce that, since the iterator itself has to change the field. It still has the first-run check.
+
+```c
+static inline bool rray_run_iterator_n_lean_next(
+  struct rray_run_iterator_n_lean* it,
+  const r_ssize n
+) {
+  if (it->end == it->size) {
+    return false;
+  }
+
+  if (it->end != 0) {
+    const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan* plan = it->plan;
+    RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(it->v_loc, it->v_point, plan, n);
+  }
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end = it->start + it->run_size;
+
+  return true;
+}
+```
+
+`rray_run_iterator_n_advance` also drops the first-run check. Its constructor already describes the first run (`it.end = it.run_size`), and the loop advances at the end of each run. That is the same shape as the macros' `while (run_start != size) { ...; RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT(); }`.
+
+```c
+static inline bool rray_run_iterator_n_advance_done(
+  const struct rray_run_iterator_n_advance* it
+) {
+  return it->start == it->size;
+}
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_n_advance_next(
+  struct rray_run_iterator_n_advance* it,
+  const r_ssize n
+) {
+  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan* plan = it->plan;
+  RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(it->v_loc, it->v_point, plan, n);
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end = it->start + it->run_size;
+}
+```
+
+The caller's loop becomes a `for` instead of a `while`:
+
+```c
+struct rray_run_iterator_n_advance it = rray_run_iterator_n_advance(plan, 2);
+
+for (; !rray_run_iterator_n_advance_done(&it);
+     rray_run_iterator_n_advance_next(&it, 2)) {
+  r_ssize x_loc = it.v_loc[0];
+  r_ssize y_loc = it.v_loc[1];
+  ...
+}
+```
+
+After the last run, `next()` carries the point back to zero one extra time. That only changes `v_point` and `v_loc`, which nothing reads afterwards. An empty array (`size == 0`) is done before the first run.
+
+## Benchmarks
+
+Both versions use exactly the same loops as `run_n`, pass the same 262 test expectations, and match `macro` with `identical()` in every case. `RRAY_MAX_INPUTS` was 64 for these runs.
+
+These were measured in a later session, when the machine ran about 7% slower overall than for the tables above (`[4000, 4000]` summed over axis 1 took 13.8 ms instead of 13.05 ms), so compare only within this table. The long-run cases were the same for every version, within noise, across two runs of the full suite. The short-run cases were then rerun on their own: 3 rounds of 50 iterations each, with the order of the versions rotated between rounds, taking the median across rounds.
+
+| Case | Macro | Run split | Run n | Lean | Advance |
+|---|---|---|---|---|---|
+| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.08 | 7.14 | 7.06 | 7.13 | 7.16 |
+| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.00 | 7.09 | 7.39 | 7.22 | 7.32 |
+| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 8.36 | 9.12 | 9.60 | 8.91 | 8.57 |
+| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 7.98 | 8.28 | 8.70 | 8.08 | 7.81 |
+| Add, integer, `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 12.08 | 12.58 | 13.13 | 13.18 | 12.02 |
+| Add, integer, `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 14.46 | 15.16 | 14.50 | 14.79 | 14.36 |
+| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.75 | 2.82 | 3.03 | 2.91 | 3.06 |
+| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 4.02 | 3.99 | 3.99 | 3.99 | 3.92 |
+
+## What the numbers say
+
+- Addition is where the per-run cost shows. `advance` is within 2.5% of the macro in all four add cases, and slightly faster in three. `run_n` was 3% to 15% slower.
+
+- Dropping the copy alone (`lean`) gets about two thirds of the way on double addition, but was mixed on integer addition. Dropping the first-run check as well is what closes the gap everywhere.
+
+- Broadcasting and the short sum don't separate the three newer iterator versions beyond noise. All of them beat the macro on the short sum along axis 1, because the total stays in a local variable.
+
+- Differences under about 3% shouldn't be read into, given the noise in this session.
+
+So the recommended iterator is the `advance` shape: one location array, advanced at the end of each run.
+

Davis

And just to double check, keeping the plan struct and the iterator structs separate is critical for performance right?

Claude

Not that I've shown. I never benchmarked a version where the plan and the iterator are merged into one struct, so the measurements don't tell us whether the split matters for speed. From what was measured, my best reading is that the split matters more for reuse than for registers.

What the measurements do show:

  • What made the iterators fast: the changing positions live in a local struct on the stack that is never passed outside the function, and next() is inlined. The compiler can then keep the positions in registers. It can also be sure that writes to v_out don't touch the iterator, because no pointer to the struct ever leaves the function.

  • That already doesn't match the header's explanation. The header in src/strided-iterator.h says the plan holds only fixed state and the caller keeps the positions so they stay in registers. The new iterators keep positions inside a struct and matched the macros anyway. So "the positions are loose locals in the caller" isn't what matters; "the positions are in something local and fully visible to the compiler" is.

What I'd expect from merging the plan into the iterator, unmeasured:

  • Probably no loss in the hot loop. The plan's dimension and stride arrays are read with a runtime axis index in RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT(), so they sit in memory either way. As long as the merged struct is still a local that doesn't leave the function, the compiler sees the same thing.

  • The cost would be copying. Each new iterator would copy the dimensions and strides into itself (one value per dimension). For a single walk that's nothing. But the nested reducer reuses one inner plan for every output element: rray_mean() walks it two or three times per output. With a merged struct, every one of those walks would recopy the plan. Today they only reset a few positions.

So I'd keep them separate, but because one plan, built once, gets walked many times. Not because a merged version would lose registers. The header comment should probably say that rather than the registers reason.

If you want it confirmed rather than reasoned, I can add a merged plan-and-iterator version to the variant package. The cases to check are the short-run cases and the nested mean, since the mean is where repeated walks of one plan would show up.

Davis

I think you should try it. I also think for mean we could just have a “reset” method that uses the same struct and resets it rather than making a new one. But I do think there will be a big performance difference

Claude

Agreed, worth measuring. I'll build a merged version where the plan and the positions are one struct, in the same "advance at the end" shape, and test it two ways:

  1. Single walk: broadcast, add and sum, against the separate-plan advance iterator.
  2. Many walks of one plan: the nested integer mean, comparing a new separate-plan iterator per output, a merged iterator reset per output (your idea), and a fresh merged iterator per output. The integer mean makes a single pass, so the per-walk setup cost isn't hidden behind a second pass.

r_obj* ffi_rray_mean(

  r_obj* ffi_axes,
  r_obj* ffi_na_rm,

) {

+struct rray_run_iterator_m {
+  r_ssize start;
+  r_ssize end;
+  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+  r_ssize v_stride[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+
+  r_ssize size;
+  r_ssize run_size;
+  int dimensionality;
+  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+};
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_m_reset(
+  struct rray_run_iterator_m* it,
+  const r_ssize n
+) {
+  it->start = 0;
+  it->end = it->run_size;
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
+    it->v_loc[i] = 0;
+  }
+
+  r_memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it->dimensionality);
+}
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_m_init(
+  struct rray_run_iterator_m* it,
+  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan* plan,
+  const r_ssize n
+) {
+  it->size = plan->size;
+  it->run_size = plan->v_dimensions[0];
+  it->dimensionality = plan->dimensionality;
+
+  for (int axis = 0; axis < plan->dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    it->v_dimensions[axis] = plan->v_dimensions[axis];
+
+    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
+      it->v_strides[axis * n + i] = plan->v_strides[axis * n + i];
+    }
+  }
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
+    it->v_stride[i] = it->v_strides[i];
+  }
+
+  rray_run_iterator_m_reset(it, n);
+}
+
+static inline bool rray_run_iterator_m_done(
+  const struct rray_run_iterator_m* it
+) {
+  return it->start == it->size;
+}
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_m_next(
+  struct rray_run_iterator_m* it,
+  const r_ssize n
+) {
+  RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(it->v_loc, it->v_point, it, n);
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end = it->start + it->run_size;
+}
+
+#endif

    def fn(s):
        m = re.search(r"static inline __attribute__\(\(always_inline\)\) r_obj\*\s+" + name + r"_advance_impl\(\n", s)
        assert m, name
        start = m.start()
        end = s.index("\n}\n", start) + 3
        impl = s[start:end]
        params = impl[impl.index("_impl(\n") + len("_impl(\n"): impl.index("\n) {")]
        wrap_params, last = params.rsplit(",\n", 1)
        merged = impl.replace(f"{name}_advance_impl(", f"{name}_merged_impl(")

        merged = merged.replace("!rray_run_iterator_n_advance_done(&it)", "!rray_run_iterator_m_done(&it)")

def mean(s):
    s = s.replace('#include "reduce.h"\n', '#include "iterator-variant.h"\n#include "reduce.h"\n#include "run-iterator-n.h"\n', 1)
    old = '''static r_obj* rray_mean_int(
  r_obj* x,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* outer_plan,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* inner_plan,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
'''
    assert s.count(old) == 1
    block = r'''static r_obj* rray_mean_int_macro(
  r_obj* x,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* outer_plan,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* inner_plan,
  struct r_lazy error_call
);

static inline __attribute__((always_inline)) double rray_mean_int_one_advance(
  const int* v_x,
  r_ssize x_start,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan* inner_plan
) {
  long double sum = 0.0;

  struct rray_run_iterator_n_advance it =
    rray_run_iterator_n_advance(inner_plan, 1);
  it.v_loc[0] = x_start;

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_n_advance_done(&it);
       rray_run_iterator_n_advance_next(&it, 1)) {
    r_ssize x_loc = it.v_loc[0];

    for (r_ssize i = it.start; i < it.end; ++i) {
      const int x_elt = v_x[x_loc];
      if (x_elt == r_globals.na_int) {
        return r_globals.na_dbl;
      }
      sum += x_elt;
      x_loc += it.v_stride[0];
    }
  }

  const r_ssize count = rray_strided_iterator_n_plan_size(inner_plan);

  return (double) (sum / count);
}

static inline __attribute__((always_inline)) double
rray_mean_int_one_merged_reset(
  const int* v_x,
  r_ssize x_start,
  struct rray_run_iterator_m* it
) {
  long double sum = 0.0;

  rray_run_iterator_m_reset(it, 1);
  it->v_loc[0] = x_start;

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(it, 1)) {
    r_ssize x_loc = it->v_loc[0];

    for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {
      const int x_elt = v_x[x_loc];
      if (x_elt == r_globals.na_int) {
        return r_globals.na_dbl;
      }
      sum += x_elt;
      x_loc += it->v_stride[0];
    }
  }

  const r_ssize count = it->size;

  return (double) (sum / count);
}

static inline __attribute__((always_inline)) double
rray_mean_int_one_merged_fresh(
  const int* v_x,
  r_ssize x_start,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan* inner_plan
) {
  long double sum = 0.0;

  struct rray_run_iterator_m it;
  rray_run_iterator_m_init(&it, inner_plan, 1);
  it.v_loc[0] = x_start;

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(&it, 1)) {
    r_ssize x_loc = it.v_loc[0];

    for (r_ssize i = it.start; i < it.end; ++i) {
      const int x_elt = v_x[x_loc];
      if (x_elt == r_globals.na_int) {
        return r_globals.na_dbl;
      }
      sum += x_elt;
      x_loc += it.v_stride[0];
    }
  }

  const r_ssize count = it.size;

  return (double) (sum / count);
}

static r_obj* rray_mean_int_advance(
  r_obj* x,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* outer_plan_1,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* inner_plan_1
) {
  r_ssize v_outer_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  r_ssize v_inner_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan outer_plan =
    rray_n_plan_from_plan(outer_plan_1, v_outer_strides);
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan inner_plan =
    rray_n_plan_from_plan(inner_plan_1, v_inner_strides);

  const r_ssize size = rray_strided_iterator_n_plan_size(&outer_plan);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(size));
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  const int* v_x = r_int_cbegin(x);

  struct rray_run_iterator_n_advance outer =
    rray_run_iterator_n_advance(&outer_plan, 1);

  for (; !rray_run_iterator_n_advance_done(&outer);
       rray_run_iterator_n_advance_next(&outer, 1)) {
    r_ssize x_loc = outer.v_loc[0];

    for (r_ssize i = outer.start; i < outer.end; ++i) {
      v_out[i] = rray_mean_int_one_advance(v_x, x_loc, &inner_plan);
      x_loc += outer.v_stride[0];
    }
  }

  FREE(1);
  return out;
}

static r_obj* rray_mean_int_merged_reset(
  r_obj* x,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* outer_plan_1,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* inner_plan_1
) {
  r_ssize v_outer_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  r_ssize v_inner_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan outer_plan =
    rray_n_plan_from_plan(outer_plan_1, v_outer_strides);
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_n_plan inner_plan =
    rray_n_plan_from_plan(inner_plan_1, v_inner_strides);

check <- function(cases, variants) {
  for (case in names(cases)) {
    f <- cases[[case]]
    set_variant(0L)
    expected <- f()
    for (variant in variants) {
      set_variant(variant)
      stopifnot(identical(f(), expected))
    }
  }
}

time <- function(cases, variants, rounds = 3L, iterations = 30L) {
  times <- array(
    NA_real_,
    c(length(cases), length(variants), rounds),
    dimnames = list(names(cases), names(variants), NULL)
  )

  for (round in seq_len(rounds)) {
    shift <- (round - 1L) %% length(variants)
    order <- c(seq(shift + 1L, length(variants)), seq_len(shift))
    for (case in names(cases)) {
      f <- cases[[case]]
      for (variant in names(variants)[order]) {
        set_variant(variants[[variant]])
        median <- bench::mark(f(), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE)$median
        times[case, variant, round] <- as.numeric(median) * 1000
      }
    }
  }

  set_variant(0L)
  round(apply(times, c(1, 2), median), 2)
}

There's no big difference. A merged plan-and-iterator struct is as fast as the separate plan in every case measured. For the mean, your reset idea is what makes merged work: building a fresh merged iterator per output costs 4% to 7% on the cases with a million outputs, and resetting removes that.

What I built in the variant package, all in the "advance at the end" shape:

  • merged: one struct holding the plan (dimensions, all strides, size) and the positions. init() copies the plan in, reset() puts it back at the start (positions and point to zero), and done() / next() work as before. With room for 64 dimensions × 64 inputs, its stride array alone is 32 KB of stack.

  • Integer mean, four versions:

    • the current macros;
    • advance: a new separate-plan iterator for each output;
    • merged_reset: one merged iterator for the inner walk, reset for each output;
    • merged_fresh: a new merged iterator for each output, copying the plan every time.

All versions pass the broadcast, add, sum and mean tests (356 expectations each) and give identical() results to the macro. Timings are the median of 3 rounds of 30 iterations, with the version order rotated between rounds.

Single walks:

Case Macro Advance Merged
Broadcast [10, 1] to [10, 1e6] 7.10 7.10 7.01
Broadcast [1, 1e6] to [10, 1e6] 6.84 7.23 7.13
Add [10, 1e6] + [10, 1] 8.38 8.40 8.52
Add [10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6] 7.89 7.88 7.95
Add, integer, [10, 1e6] + [10, 1] 12.00 11.84 12.00
Add [4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000] 10.89 10.54 10.71
Sum [10, 1e6] over 1 3.68 2.88 3.05

Sum [4000, 4000] over 1 13.80 13.80 13.78
Sum [4000, 4000] over 2 2.44 2.42 2.40

Integer mean, one inner walk per output:

Case Outputs Macro Advance Merged, reset Merged, fresh
[4000, 4000] over 1 4000 14.16 14.17 14.15 14.15
[4000, 4000] over 2 4000 43.25 42.84 41.65 43.01
[10, 1e6] over 1 1e6 8.17 7.95 7.76 8.45
[1e6, 10] over 2 1e6 8.16 7.91 7.87 8.51
[200, 200, 200] over 1, 3 200 7.60 7.58 7.73 7.63
[10, 1e5, 10] over 1, 3 1e5 6.61 6.50 6.54 6.64

What this shows:

  • For a single walk, merged and separate perform the same. The differences are within the roughly 3% noise of this session, and move in both directions. The short sum is the largest gap (3.05 against 2.88 ms), but advance alone has ranged from 2.85 to 3.06 ms across sessions on that case.

  • Copying the plan per walk is the only cost found. merged_fresh is 4% to 7% slower than the others when there are a million outputs, and the same when there are few. Resetting avoids the copy, and then merged matches the separate plan.

  • So performance doesn't decide whether to keep them separate. The 32 KB stack cost of a merged struct showed no measurable effect either.

What's left is a design question:

  • Separate: the plan is built once and can be shared, for example one inner plan by several walkers. The plan's arrays are sized for the real number of inputs, through a pointer, rather than for the maximum.

  • Merged: one object to create, with a reset(), and nothing extra to pass around.

Two caveats. This is one machine and one compiler (Apple clang on arm64). And the mean kernels are simple loops that don't vectorize much. I wouldn't expect either to change the conclusion, since the struct layout doesn't touch the inner loop, but I haven't checked.

Want me to add this to plans/iterators.md as a "Plan and iterator: separate or merged" section and commit and push it?

Davis

And was this build in rray itself? Like did you have to thread through a bunch of parameters so that the core loop builds the iterator locally and it doesn’t escape the function?

Claude

Yes, it was built into rray4 itself, and no extra parameters needed threading through. But that answer comes with a caveat that weakens my last conclusion.

How it's wired in the variant package:

  • Every timing goes through the real entry points: rray_broadcast(), rray_add(), rray_sum() and rray_mean(), with the existing drivers unchanged. The drivers are rray_broadcast() itself, rray_binary_arithmetic(), rray_reduce() and rray_reduce_nested().

  • Each driver already builds the plan, picks the per-type kernel with the type switch, and calls it through a function pointer with const plan*. Every version I added lives inside that kernel, like rray_sum_dbl(). It builds its iterator there as a local, from the plan pointer it already receives.

  • Within the kernel, the iterator's address only goes to static inline functions (done(), next(), the mean's per-output function). Those are inlined, so the iterator never leaves the kernel. Where I split a body into a shared _impl, I forced inlining, after the earlier run_n lesson.

So nothing had to be threaded through, because the plan pointer was already there. That's what made "the iterator doesn't leave the function" come for free.

The caveat: my "merged" version was also built inside the kernel, by copying from the plan the driver had already built. So the test really measured "a merged struct built locally inside the kernel". A real merged design wouldn't look like that. The driver would build the merged object (coalescing included) and hand it to the kernel. The kernel is called through a function pointer, so that hand-off can't be inlined, and the kernel would then have two choices:

  1. Copy it into a local first. That's the merged_fresh cost: plan arrays plus positions. Done naively it's worse, since memcpy() of the whole struct is about 33 KB with the 64 × 64 stride array. The plan alone is also about 33 KB, but it's only read through a pointer and never copied.

  2. Work through the pointer. Then the positions live in the driver's stack frame, behind a pointer the kernel can't see the origin of. When the kernel writes to v_out, the compiler has to consider whether that write changed an iterator field. For double and int outputs, C's type rules mostly rule that out. For raw outputs (unsigned char) they don't, since a char pointer may point at anything. The int dimensionality field could also be suspect for int outputs. Any field it can't rule out gets reloaded from memory instead of staying in a register.

So the real answer to your earlier question is: keeping the plan separate isn't needed for speed inside one function, which is what I measured. It does matter at the driver-to-kernel boundary. A small fixed plan, passed as a const pointer, lets each kernel build its own changing state locally with almost no setup. A merged object would have to be copied or used through a pointer. That's likely the performance difference you were expecting, and my test didn't exercise it.

I can measure that too: build the merged struct in the driver, pass it to the kernel, and compare "copy into a local" against "work through the pointer". Raw broadcast would be the case most likely to show the type-rules problem. Want me to?

Davis

Claude

I'll add the hand-off: the driver builds the merged struct and passes a pointer to the kernel, which can't be inlined into the driver. The kernel then either copies the struct into a local, or works through the pointer. A third version works through the pointer but reads each run's fields into locals first, the obvious fix if the pointer version is slow. I'll cover raw broadcast too, since raw output is where C's type rules can't help.

import sys, os
root = sys.argv[1]

def bcast_body(ctype, alloc, cbegin, begin, locals_):
    if not locals_:
        loop = f'''  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(it, 1)) {{
    if (it->v_stride[0] == 0) {{
      const {ctype} x_elt = v_x[it->v_loc[0]];

      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {{
        v_out[i] = x_elt;
      }}
    }} else {{
      r_ssize x_loc = it->v_loc[0];

      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {{
        v_out[i] = v_x[x_loc];
        x_loc += it->v_stride[0];
      }}
    }}
  }}
'''
    else:
        loop = f'''  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(it, 1)) {{
    const r_ssize start = it->start;
    const r_ssize end = it->end;
    const r_ssize x_stride = it->v_stride[0];
    r_ssize x_loc = it->v_loc[0];

    if (x_stride == 0) {{
      const {ctype} x_elt = v_x[x_loc];

      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {{
        v_out[i] = x_elt;
      }}
    }} else {{
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {{
        v_out[i] = v_x[x_loc];
        x_loc += x_stride;
      }}
    }}
  }}
'''
    return f'''  const r_ssize size = it->size;

  r_obj* out = KEEP({alloc}(size));
  {ctype}* v_out = {begin}(out);

  const {ctype}* v_x = {cbegin}(x);

{loop}
  FREE(1);
  return out;
'''

def add_body(locals_):
    if not locals_:
        loop = '''  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(it, 2)) {
    r_ssize x_loc = it->v_loc[0];
    r_ssize y_loc = it->v_loc[1];

    if (it->v_stride[0] == 0 && it->v_stride[1] == 0) {
      const double elt = rray_add_dbl_one(v_x[x_loc], v_y[y_loc]);
      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = elt;
      }
    } else if (it->v_stride[0] == 0) {
      const double x_elt = v_x[x_loc];
      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = rray_add_dbl_one(x_elt, v_y[y_loc]);
        y_loc += it->v_stride[1];
      }
    } else if (it->v_stride[1] == 0) {
      const double y_elt = v_y[y_loc];
      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = rray_add_dbl_one(v_x[x_loc], y_elt);
        x_loc += it->v_stride[0];
      }
    } else {
      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = rray_add_dbl_one(v_x[x_loc], v_y[y_loc]);
        x_loc += it->v_stride[0];
        y_loc += it->v_stride[1];
      }
    }
  }
'''
    else:
        loop = '''  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(it, 2)) {
    const r_ssize start = it->start;
    const r_ssize end = it->end;
    const r_ssize x_stride = it->v_stride[0];
    const r_ssize y_stride = it->v_stride[1];
    r_ssize x_loc = it->v_loc[0];
    r_ssize y_loc = it->v_loc[1];

    if (x_stride == 0 && y_stride == 0) {
      const double elt = rray_add_dbl_one(v_x[x_loc], v_y[y_loc]);
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = elt;
      }
    } else if (x_stride == 0) {
      const double x_elt = v_x[x_loc];
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = rray_add_dbl_one(x_elt, v_y[y_loc]);
        y_loc += y_stride;
      }
    } else if (y_stride == 0) {
      const double y_elt = v_y[y_loc];
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = rray_add_dbl_one(v_x[x_loc], y_elt);
        x_loc += x_stride;
      }
    } else {
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = rray_add_dbl_one(v_x[x_loc], v_y[y_loc]);
        x_loc += x_stride;
        y_loc += y_stride;
      }
    }
  }
'''
    return f'''  const r_ssize size = it->size;

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(size));
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);
  const double* v_y = r_dbl_cbegin(y);

{loop}
  FREE(1);
  return out;
'''

def sum_body(locals_):
    if not locals_:
        loop = '''  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(it, 1)) {
    if (it->v_stride[0] == 0) {
      double sum = v_out[it->v_loc[0]];

      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {
        sum = rray_sum_dbl_one(sum, v_x[i]);
      }

      v_out[it->v_loc[0]] = sum;
    } else {
      r_ssize out_loc = it->v_loc[0];

      for (r_ssize i = it->start; i < it->end; ++i) {
        v_out[out_loc] = rray_sum_dbl_one(v_out[out_loc], v_x[i]);
        out_loc += it->v_stride[0];
      }
    }
  }
'''
    else:
        loop = '''  for (; !rray_run_iterator_m_done(it); rray_run_iterator_m_next(it, 1)) {
    const r_ssize start = it->start;
    const r_ssize end = it->end;
    const r_ssize out_stride = it->v_stride[0];
    r_ssize out_loc = it->v_loc[0];

    if (out_stride == 0) {
      double sum = v_out[out_loc];

      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
        sum = rray_sum_dbl_one(sum, v_x[i]);
      }

      v_out[out_loc] = sum;
    } else {
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {
        v_out[out_loc] = rray_sum_dbl_one(v_out[out_loc], v_x[i]);
        out_loc += out_stride;
      }
    }
  }
'''
    return f'''  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(out_size));
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {{
    v_out[i] = 0.0;
  }}

  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);

{loop}
  FREE(1);
  return out;
'''

def mean_body(locals_):
    if not locals_:
        inner = '''      long double sum = 0.0;
      bool na = false;

      rray_run_iterator_m_reset(inner, 1);

Yes, there's a big difference once the merged object comes from the driver and the kernel works through the pointer. Raw broadcast gets up to 5x slower, and the strided mean 11% slower. Reading each run's fields into locals first removes the slowdown. That makes the separate plan the safer default: it gives the fast version without anyone having to remember a rule.

In the variant package, the drivers (rray_broadcast(), rray_binary_arithmetic(), rray_reduce() and rray_reduce_nested()) now build the merged struct and pass a pointer to the kernel. The kernel lives in another file and is marked noinline, which matches today's function-pointer call from driver to kernel. Three kernel versions:

  • copy: copies the struct into a local (struct rray_run_iterator_m it_local = *p_it;), then runs the same loops as the local version.

  • pointer: uses the driver's struct through the pointer, with the same loop text (it->end, it->v_stride[0] and so on).

  • pointer_locals: uses the pointer, but reads start, end, the strides and the starting locations into locals at the top of each run.

All three pass the broadcast, add, sum and mean tests (356 expectations each) and give identical() results to the macro. Timings are the median of 3 rounds of 30 iterations, with the order rotated between rounds. "Local" is last round's merged struct built inside the kernel.

Raw broadcast:

Case Macro Copy Pointer Pointer, locals
[10, 1] to [10, 1e6] 3.35 2.86 5.47 3.16
[1, 1e6] to [10, 1e6] 3.44 3.22 4.80 3.13
[4000, 1] to [4000, 4000] 1.23 1.25 6.46 1.43

Integer mean:

Case Macro Local Copy Pointer Pointer, locals
[4000, 4000] over 1 14.12 14.15 14.15 14.15 14.16
[4000, 4000] over 2 42.77 42.82 43.00 47.71 43.12
[10, 1e6] over 1 7.92 7.88 8.44 7.26 6.99
[1e6, 10] over 2 7.87 7.87 8.45 7.26 6.98
[200, 200, 200] over 1, 3 7.67 7.59 7.93 7.60 7.64
[10, 1e5, 10] over 1, 3 6.60 6.51 7.17 7.20 7.09

Double broadcast, add and sum:

Case Macro Local Copy Pointer Pointer, locals
Sum [10, 1e6] over 1 3.90 2.76 3.60 2.89 2.72
Add [10, 1e6] + [10, 1] 8.24 8.49 8.25 8.44 8.67
Add [4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000] 10.63 10.90 10.61 10.48 10.70

The other double cases were within about 3% of each other across all five versions.

What this shows:

  • Working through the pointer is the trap. For raw output, every byte written might, as far as C's rules go, have changed it->end or it->v_stride[0]. So the compiler reloads them on every element and can't vectorize. That's 5.2x slower on [4000, 1] to [4000, 4000]. Double output isn't affected, because C's type rules let the compiler assume a double write can't change an r_ssize field. That's why it never showed up in the double tests. The strided mean also lost 11% through the pointer.

  • Reading each run's fields into locals fixes it everywhere, and was the fastest version on the short-walk means (6.98 ms against 7.87 for the macro). But it's a rule every loop has to follow. Forgetting it costs nothing visible on double and only bites on raw, or anything else written through a char-sized type.

  • Copying the struct into a local also avoids the raw problem, but has its own costs that I can't explain. It was 30% slower on the short sum and 7% to 10% slower on the short-walk means. The copy happens once per call (about 33 KB, microseconds), so the copy itself can't account for milliseconds. My guess is that clang optimizes the loops less well after copying such a large struct, but I haven't checked.

So, to your original question: the split is not strictly needed for speed. The pointer-plus-locals merged version proves careful code can match it. What the split does is make the fast path the default. The kernel gets a small const plan, which is only read between runs, and builds its changing positions as locals that can't be confused with the output. There's no rule to remember and no type-dependent slowdown to discover later. That's a good reason to keep it. The header comment could say exactly that.

Read plans/iterators.md 
17	- That design is what NumPy does. It is also what the macros already do. The loops are the same, so the speed is the same. What changes is where the loops live: in each function, where they can be read and changed, instead of inside a macro.
18	
19	- One run iterator can serve any number of inputs, replacing today's separate one-input and two-input plans. It's as fast as the specialized ones, as long as each function passes the number of inputs as a literal (`1`, `2`) and the call is inlined. With the count known only at runtime, short runs get 25% to 75% slower.
20	
21	- The short-run gap goes away when the iterator does less per run: keep one location array that the iterator advances directly (no per-run copy for the caller), and advance at the end of each run instead of checking for the first run at the start, like the macros do. That version is within 2.5% of the macro on every short-run case.
22	
23	- The cost is repetition. rray4 has 84 `RRAY_BINARY()` calls, 32 `RRAY_REDUCE()` calls and 5 `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` calls. Writing every stride case out by hand in each of them is a lot of code. How to handle the per-type repetition is the main open question.
24	
25	Recommendation: adopt one run iterator for any number of inputs, in the "advance at the end" shape, with per-stride-case loops written in each function. Decide separately how to avoid writing every type out by hand.
26	
27	---

-# The cost: repetition
+So the recommended iterator is the `advance` shape: one location array, advanced at the end of each run.
+
+---
+
+# Plan and iterator: separate or merged
+
+Every iterator above keeps two structs: an immutable plan (dimensions, strides, size), built once by the driver and passed to the kernel as a `const` pointer, and an iterator holding the changing positions, built inside the kernel. This section checks whether that split matters, or whether one merged struct would do.
+
+## How the variant package is wired
+
+- Every timing goes through the real entry points (`rray_broadcast()`, `rray_add()`, `rray_sum()`, `rray_mean()`) and their unchanged drivers (`rray_broadcast()` itself, `rray_binary_arithmetic()`, `rray_reduce()`, `rray_reduce_nested()`).
+
+- Each driver already builds the plan, picks the per-type kernel, and calls it through a function pointer with `const plan*`. Every iterator version above is built inside that kernel, as a local, from the plan pointer it already receives. Its address only goes to `static inline` functions (`done()`, `next()`), so it never leaves the kernel, and the compiler can keep its positions in registers. No extra parameters needed threading through.
+
+## The merged struct
+
+```c
+struct rray_run_iterator_m {
+  r_ssize start;
+  r_ssize end;
+  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+  r_ssize v_stride[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+
+  r_ssize size;
+  r_ssize run_size;
+  int dimensionality;
+  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+};
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_m_reset(
+  struct rray_run_iterator_m* it,
+  const r_ssize n
+) {
+  it->start = 0;
+  it->end = it->run_size;
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
+    it->v_loc[i] = 0;
+  }
+
+  r_memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it->dimensionality);
+}
+```
+
+`rray_run_iterator_m_init()` copies the dimensions and strides from a plan, then calls `reset()`. `done()` and `next()` are the same as the `advance` iterator, with the plan fields read from the struct itself. With room for 64 dimensions and 64 inputs, the stride array alone is 32 KB.
+
+## Round 1: merged, built inside the kernel
+
+The kernel builds the merged struct itself, copying from the plan the driver passed in. For the nested integer mean (one inner walk per output), three ways to handle the inner walk:
+
+- `advance`: a new separate-plan iterator for each output.
+
+- `merged_reset`: one merged iterator, reset for each output.
+
+- `merged_fresh`: a new merged iterator for each output, copying the plan every time.
+
+The integer mean makes a single pass, so the per-walk setup cost isn't hidden behind a second pass. Median of 3 rounds of 30 iterations, with the order of the versions rotated between rounds.
+
+| Case | Macro | Advance | Merged |
+|---|---|---|---|
+| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.10 | 7.10 | 7.01 |
+| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 6.84 | 7.23 | 7.13 |
+| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 8.38 | 8.40 | 8.52 |
+| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 7.89 | 7.88 | 7.95 |
+| Add, integer, `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 12.00 | 11.84 | 12.00 |
+| Add `[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]` | 10.89 | 10.54 | 10.71 |
+| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.68 | 2.88 | 3.05 |
+| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 4.01 | 3.92 | 3.98 |
+| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 13.80 | 13.80 | 13.78 |
+| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 2.44 | 2.42 | 2.40 |
+
+| Integer mean | Outputs | Macro | Advance | Merged, reset | Merged, fresh |
+|---|---|---|---|---|---|
+| `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 4000 | 14.16 | 14.17 | 14.15 | 14.15 |
+| `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 4000 | 43.25 | 42.84 | 41.65 | 43.01 |
+| `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 1e6 | 8.17 | 7.95 | 7.76 | 8.45 |
+| `[1e6, 10]` over 2 | 1e6 | 8.16 | 7.91 | 7.87 | 8.51 |
+| `[200, 200, 200]` over 1, 3 | 200 | 7.60 | 7.58 | 7.73 | 7.63 |
+| `[10, 1e5, 10]` over 1, 3 | 1e5 | 6.61 | 6.50 | 6.54 | 6.64 |
+
+- For a single walk, merged and separate are the same within this session's noise (about 3%).
+
+- Copying the plan for every walk costs 4% to 7% when there are a million outputs. Resetting one merged iterator avoids that copy and matches the separate plan.
+
+This round only shows that merging costs nothing inside one function. A real merged design wouldn't build the struct in the kernel, though. The driver would build it and hand it over.
+
+## Round 2: merged, built by the driver
+
+The drivers build the merged struct and pass a pointer to the kernel. The kernels live in their own file and are marked `noinline`, matching today's function-pointer call from driver to kernel. Three kernel versions:
+
+- `copy`: copies the struct into a local (`struct rray_run_iterator_m it_local = *p_it;`), then runs the same loops as round 1.
+
+- `pointer`: uses the driver's struct through the pointer, with the same loop text (`it->end`, `it->v_stride[0]`, ...).
+
+- `pointer_locals`: uses the pointer, but reads `start`, `end`, the strides and the starting locations into locals at the top of each run.
+
+"Local" is round 1's merged struct built inside the kernel. Same timing method as round 1.
+
+| Raw broadcast | Macro | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
+|---|---|---|---|---|
+| `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 3.35 | 2.86 | 5.47 | 3.16 |
+| `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 3.44 | 3.22 | 4.80 | 3.13 |
+| `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]` | 1.23 | 1.25 | 6.46 | 1.43 |
+
+| Integer mean | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
+|---|---|---|---|---|---|
+| `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 14.12 | 14.15 | 14.15 | 14.15 | 14.16 |
+| `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 42.77 | 42.82 | 43.00 | 47.71 | 43.12 |
+| `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 7.92 | 7.88 | 8.44 | 7.26 | 6.99 |
+| `[1e6, 10]` over 2 | 7.87 | 7.87 | 8.45 | 7.26 | 6.98 |
+| `[200, 200, 200]` over 1, 3 | 7.67 | 7.59 | 7.93 | 7.60 | 7.64 |
+| `[10, 1e5, 10]` over 1, 3 | 6.60 | 6.51 | 7.17 | 7.20 | 7.09 |
+
+| Double | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
+|---|---|---|---|---|---|
+| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.12 | 7.30 | 6.79 | 6.87 | 7.16 |
+| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.15 | 7.16 | 7.02 | 7.14 | 7.03 |
+| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 8.24 | 8.49 | 8.25 | 8.44 | 8.67 |
+| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 8.04 | 7.88 | 7.73 | 7.71 | 7.65 |
+| Add `[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]` | 10.63 | 10.90 | 10.61 | 10.48 | 10.70 |
+| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.90 | 2.76 | 3.60 | 2.89 | 2.72 |
+| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 3.99 | 3.94 | 3.93 | 3.99 | 3.92 |
+| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 13.78 | 13.78 | 13.76 | 13.78 | 13.79 |
+| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 2.41 | 2.39 | 2.41 | 2.43 | 2.42 |
+
+## What the numbers say
+
+- Working through the pointer is the trap. For raw output, C allows a byte written through `Rbyte*` to change any object, including `it->end` and `it->v_stride[0]`. So the compiler reloads them on every element and can't vectorize: 5.2x slower on `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]`. Double output isn't affected, because C's type rules let the compiler assume a `double` write can't change an `r_ssize` field. That's why it never showed up in the double tests. The strided mean also lost 11% through the pointer.
+
+- Reading each run's fields into locals fixes it everywhere, and was the fastest version on the short-walk means (6.98 ms against 7.87 ms for the macro). But it's a rule every loop has to follow. Forgetting it costs nothing visible on `double` and only bites on raw, or anything else written through a character type.
+
+- Copying the struct into a local also avoids the raw problem, but was 30% slower on the short sum and 7% to 10% slower on the short-walk means. The copy happens once per call (about 33 KB, microseconds), so it can't account for milliseconds by itself. A guess is that clang optimizes the loops less well after copying such a large struct. It hasn't been looked into.
+
+- The separate plan avoids all of this without a rule. The kernel gets a small `const` plan, which is only read between runs, and builds its changing positions as locals that can't be confused with the output.
+
+So keep the plan and the iterator separate. The reason isn't that merging is slower in itself. It's that the split makes the fast version the default. The header comment in `src/strided-iterator.h` should give that reason instead of "positions managed by the caller stay in registers", since round 1 shows an iterator struct keeps them in registers just as well.
+
+For the nested reducer, the inner iterator should get a `reset()` that puts it back at the start of the plan, with a new starting location, instead of building a new iterator for every output.
+
+---
+
